Two biochemical settings have been studied within the linear framework. In the first setting, the system is a macroscopic bulk mixture of biochemical components interacting through chemical reactions. Here, the variables correspond to the concentrations of the components, which evolve deterministically over time, as described by the Laplacian dynamics in Eqn. 1. A typical example is a network of enzymes and substrates; the linear framework was originally introduced to analyse such systems (Gunawardena 2012). For related applications in the macroscopic setting, see Dasgupta et al. (2014); Nam et al. (2020); Yordanov and Stelling (2018, 2020); and for a review, see Gunawardena (2014).

In the second setting, the system is a microscopic one, typically consisting of an individual macromolecule, such as a gene. The macromolecule evolves stochastically among a collection of states, partly through interactions with external components in the surrounding thermal bath. The vertices of the graph represent the states of the macromolecule, the edges represent transitions among these states, and the edge labels represent transition rates. The components in the environment are often assumed to be present in such abundance, in thermodynamic “reservoirs”, that their concentrations can be treated as constant during interactions with the graph. In this case, the edge labels are constants. This specification gives rise to a finite-state, continuous-time, time-homogeneous Markov process, X(t). Each variable, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i(t)$$\end{document}xi(t), in Eqn. 1 corresponds to the probability of the system occupying state i at time t; and there is an edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \rightarrow j$$\end{document}i→j if, and only if, the infinitesimal transition rate from i to j is positive,3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{h \rightarrow 0}{\frac{\Pr {\left[ X(t + h) = j \mid X(t) = i \right] }}{h}} > 0 \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limh→0PrX(t+h)=j∣X(t)=ih>0,and this positive rate is the corresponding edge label, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell (i \rightarrow j)$$\end{document}ℓ(i→j). Any finite-state, time-homogeneous Markov process for which the infinitesimal transition rates are well-defined can be described by a linear framework graph in this way (Mirzaev and Gunawardena 2013). (In some probability textbooks (Anderson 1991; Norris 1997), the infinitesimal generator of the Markov process is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)^\textrm{T}$$\end{document}L(G)T and is called the “Q-matrix.”) Gene regulation is a biological context in which the microscopic interpretation of the linear framework has been particularly useful (Ahsendorf et al. 2014; Estrada et al. 2016; Biddle et al. 2019; Park et al. 2019; Martinez-Corral et al. 2024; Nasser et al. 2024); for a review, see Wong and Gunawardena (2020).

In the microscopic interpretation, the Laplacian dynamics in Eqn. 1 is the master equation, or Kolmogorov forward equation, associated with X(t) (van Kampen 2007), which describes the deterministic time evolution of the probability distribution on the states in the process. It is an interesting fact that both the macroscopic and microscopic contexts can be described by the same Laplacian dynamics in Eqn. 1. In this paper, we will focus on the microscopic setting, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_i(t)$$\end{document}xi(t) will denote probability rather than concentration, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_{\text {tot}} = 1$$\end{document}xtot=1. We will accordingly be studying Markov processes from a graph-theoretic perspective.

Up to now, the focus of the linear framework has been on the steady state, which is appropriate for the timescale separations that are frequently used in analysing biochemical systems (Gunawardena 2014). It follows from Eqn. 1 that the steady state, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{x}^*$$\end{document}x∗, lies in the kernel of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)$$\end{document}L(G). A basis for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ker \mathcal {L}(G)$$\end{document}kerL(G) can be described in terms of the edge labels of G by the Matrix-Tree theorem (MTT) (Gunawardena 2012; Mirzaev and Gunawardena 2013); see Theorem 1. When G is strongly connected, meaning that there is a directed path of edges from each vertex to every other vertex in G, then (Gunawardena 2012),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \dim {\ker {\mathcal {L}(G)}} = 1 \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dimkerL(G)=1.The MTT shows that a canonical basis vector, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\rho }(G) \in \ker {\mathcal {L}(G)}$$\end{document}ρ(G)∈kerL(G), is given by4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho _i(G) = \sum _{F \in \Phi _{\{i\}}(G)}{\left( \prod _{j \rightarrow k \in F}{\ell (j \rightarrow k)} \right) } \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρi(G)=∑F∈Φ{i}(G)∏j→k∈Fℓ(j→k),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\{i\}}(G)$$\end{document}Φ{i}(G) is the set of spanning trees of G that are rooted at i. A spanning tree, F, of G is a subgraph of G which includes each vertex (“spanning”), has no cycles if edge directions are ignored (“tree”), and has exactly one outgoing edge from each vertex except one, which has no outgoing edges; this special vertex is the root of F. For instance, the graph in Fig. 1A, which is strongly connected, has 20 spanning trees that are rooted at the vertex 1, each of which contributes a product of four edge labels to the sum in Eqn. 4 (Fig. 1B). We can obtain the steady-state probability of vertex i from Eqn. 4 by normalising to the total,5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x_i^* = \frac{\rho _i(G)}{\rho _1(G) + \cdots + \rho _N(G)} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}xi∗=ρi(G)ρ1(G)+⋯+ρN(G).The case of a general, non-strongly connected graph is discussed in Mirzaev and Gunawardena (2013). Importantly, Eqns. 4 and 5 are symbolic in the edge labels and do not require the numerical values of the transition rates to be known.

Since the Laplacian dynamics in Eqn. 1 is linear, the steady state is an eigenvector for the zero eigenvalue. This eigenvector can be readily obtained from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal {L}}}(G)$$\end{document}L(G) but the resulting determinant formulas contain alternating signs. The MTT is important because it accounts for the remarkable cancellations that allow each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _i(G)$$\end{document}ρi(G) to be expressed as a manifestly positive polynomial in the edge labels (Eqn. 4). (The distinction here is that although the polynomial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a^2 - 2ab + b^2 = (a-b)^2$$\end{document}a2-2ab+b2=(a-b)2 is always positive when a and b are positive and unequal, it is not a sum of monomials with positive coefficients, which is “manifestly” positive.) Indeed, Eqn. 4 yields a sum of monomials whose coefficients are all positive integers, in this case the same integer, 1. Eqn. 5 then shows that steady-state probabilities are also rational algebraic functions of the edge labels with the same property. Positivity would be expected for the functional dependency of the probability on the edge labels but the manifest (integral) positivity is an additional mathematical feature. It arises because the steady-state probabilities of a Markov process are not merely probabilities. They are also governed by an underlying combinatorial structure, derived from the spanning trees of the corresponding linear framework graph, as expressed by the MTT (Eqn. 4). Manifest positivity also arises in other contexts; see Williams (2022) for more discussion. The existence within the steady state of an underlying combinatorial structure, in the form of spanning trees, from which the manifest positivity arises, is a mathematical feature that has been central to the linear framework.

Another significant consequence of the MTT that emerges from Eqn. 4 is that steady-state probabilities can be calculated irrespective of the thermodynamic context. If the graph can reach a steady state of thermodynamic equilibrium, so that detailed balance, or the cycle condition, is satisfied, then Eqn. 5 reduces to the standard prescription of equilibrium statistical mechanics, with the denominator corresponding to the partition function for the grand canonical ensemble (Gunawardena 2012; Ahsendorf et al. 2014; Estrada et al. 2016). However, Eqns. 4 and 5 hold equally well in a steady state away from thermodynamic equilibrium. The linear framework thereby provides a restricted context in which non-equilibrium statistical mechanics is exactly solvable. This capability has been exploited in several recent applications (Estrada et al. 2016; Biddle et al. 2019; Park et al. 2019; Martinez-Corral et al. 2024); for a review in the context of gene regulation, see Wong and Gunawardena (2020).

The use of graph-theoretic methods in stochastic thermodynamics goes back to the work of Hill (1966) and Schnakenberg (1976) and has been resurrected more recently; see, for example, Andrieux and Gaspard (2007); Murugan et al. (2014); Owen and Horowitz (2023); Dal Cengio et al. (2023); Qureshi et al. (2023). For biochemical systems, the graph theory approach of the linear framework allows appropriate molecular details to be left unspecified, by imposing purely topological requirements, while the rational algebraic access through Eqn. 5, which does not require parameter values to be known, has allowed rigorous theorems to be proved that rise above the underlying molecular complexity (Wong et al. 2018; Biddle et al. 2021; Martinez-Corral et al. 2024).

The linear framework has been successful in analysing systems at steady state but it had long been thought that it could not be extended to the transient regime, during which the system is relaxing to a steady state. It seemed that this regime could only be accessed through the eigenvalues of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)$$\end{document}L(G). The main contribution of the present paper is to show that this is not the case. It is possible to express certain transient quantities, such as the moments of the first-passage time (FPT) distribution, as manifestly positive rational algebraic functions of the edge labels. This capability opens up a large class of new problems that can potentially be addressed in a similar way to the applications described above.

The FPT from one vertex, i, to another, j, is the random variable measuring the time taken by a trajectory of the associated Markov process to reach j for the first time, when started from i. FPTs have been used to quantify a variety of observables in molecular biophysics: the completion time of an enzymatic turnover (Kou et al. 2005; Shaevitz et al. 2005; Kolomeisky and Fisher 2007; Chemla et al. 2008; Garai et al. 2009; Bel et al. 2010; Moffitt et al. 2010; Moffitt and Bustamante 2014), the probability and speed with which an enzyme can choose between correct and incorrect reaction pathways (Banerjee et al. 2017; Cui and Mehta 2018; Mallory et al. 2019; Wang et al. 2021), the speed with which a gene alternates between different regulatory states (Lammers et al. 2020; Alamos et al. 2023; Lammers et al. 2023), the time taken by a regulated molecule to cross an abundance threshold (Dal Co et al. 2017; Ghusinga et al. 2017; Gupta et al. 2018; Ham et al. 2024), and the rate of templated copolymer growth (Qureshi et al. 2023; Guntoro et al. 2025). However, despite their broad usefulness in biology, FPTs have usually been calculated numerically or by ad hoc methods for special cases (Bel et al. 2010), or by more general methods for which closed-form solutions are not readily found (Shaevitz et al. 2005; Chemla et al. 2008). Even when rational functions emerge in such treatments, their manifest positivity may not always be visible (Bel et al. 2010). The use of Terrell Hill’s method for calculating mean FPTs (Hill 1988), as in Qureshi et al. (2023); Guntoro et al. (2025), is an exception to which we will return below.

In this paper, we introduce a systematic graph-theoretic treatment of FPTs for Markov processes, and thereby extend the scope of the linear framework from the steady state to the transient regime. To do this, we exploit a generalisation of the MTT, the All-Minors Matrix-Tree theorem (AMMTT), stated here as Theorem 1. The AMMTT uses spanning forests, which generalise the spanning trees of the classical MTT described above. The AMMTT enables us to provide closed-form solutions for the moments of any FPT distribution, either unconditional or conditional, along with related quantities such as splitting probabilities, as manifestly positive rational algebraic functions of the edge labels.

The AMMTT has been used to derive formulas for mean FPTs (Chebotarev 2007; Pitman and Tang 2018) and for splitting probabilities (Pitman and Tang 2018) in discrete-time Markov chains, but not, to our knowledge, for higher moments and conditional FPTs of the continuous-time Markov processes studied here.

Generalisations of the classical work on FPTs of Hill (1988) and of Kac (1947) can be shown to follow from the results presented here. For reasons of space, we discuss these connections in a sequel to the present paper (Nam and Gunawardena 2025). The main application of our results so far has been to the analysis of a tradeoff between accuracy and speed in the CRISPR–Cas9 system, which will also be presented elsewhere (Nam et al. 2025).

We begin with some preliminary definitions and results; a summary of the various symbols and notation that we use is also provided in Table 1. The AMMTT is introduced in Theorem 1. We deduce an important consequence of this in Proposition 2, which shows how the inverse of a principal submatrix of the Laplacian matrix may be calculated in terms of spanning forests. This technical result is a key ingredient in what follows. We then focus on the special case of graphs in which the target vertices for FPTs are the “terminal” vertices in the graph, in a sense explained below. This special case allows a differential equation to be formulated for the time evolution of the probabilities of reaching the target vertices (Eqn. 34). The Laplace transform of this adjoint master equation allows the moments of the appropriate FPT distribution to be calculated in a standard way (Eqn. 42). This moment calculation requires the inverse of a principal submatrix of the Laplacian matrix (Eqn. 44), which Proposition 2 describes in terms of spanning forests (Eqn. 47). It is then straightforward to deduce our main results: Theorem 4 for the splitting probabilities, Theorem 5 for the moments of the conditional FPT to a specific target vertex, and Theorem 6 for the moments of the unconditional FPT to any target vertex. We then explain how FPTs may be calculated in a general graph by modifying the graph so that it falls into the special case on which the previous analysis was based. Finally, we work through some examples, of pipeline graphs (Fig. 4) and of a butterfly graph (Fig. 5), both taken from the literature, to illustrate how the methods introduced here can be used in practice.

The work described here was begun in K-MN’s Harvard PhD thesis (Nam 2021), under the supervision of JG. The present paper uses a more direct proof strategy and fills in some details that were omitted in Nam (2021). An overview of this material, without proofs but with examples, appeared in Nam and Gunawardena (2023), which should be consulted for further background.

Results

Background and preliminaries

Graphs and spanning forests

We begin by establishing some notation and terminology. See the Table in the Appendix for a summary. We will continue to use the notation in the Introduction, with decorations where necessary, as in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \rightarrow _G j$$\end{document}i→Gj, to specify which graph, G, is intended. We assume that all graphs are connected when edge directions are ignored, so that they do not break up into separated pieces.

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}i∈V(G), let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{V}_{\text {out}}(i) \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}Vout(i)⊆V(G) denote the set of vertices to which i has an edge, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{V}_{\text {out}}(i) = \left\{ j \in \mathcal {V}(G) : i \rightarrow j \right\} $$\end{document}Vout(i)=j∈V(G):i→j, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{V}_{\text {in}}(i) \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}Vin(i)⊆V(G) denote the set of vertices which have an edge to i, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{V}_{\text {in}}(i) = \left\{ j \in \mathcal {V}(G) : j \rightarrow i \right\} $$\end{document}Vin(i)=j∈V(G):j→i.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A, B \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G) = \left\{ 1, \dotsc , N \right\} $$\end{document}A,B⊆V(G)=1,⋯,N be vertex subsets. We denote the size of A by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\# A$$\end{document}#A and the complement of A by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{A}}= \mathcal {V}(G) \setminus A$$\end{document}A¯=V(G)\A. Given a matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}\in \mathbb {R}^{N \times N}$$\end{document}M∈RN×N, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}_{[A,B]} \in \mathbb {R}^{\# A \times \# B}$$\end{document}M[A,B]∈R#A×#B the submatrix consisting of the rows indexed by A and the columns indexed by B. We use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{I}$$\end{document}I to denote the identity matrix, leaving its dimensions to be inferred from context.

Let H be a subgraph of G. The weight of H, denoted w(H), is the product of the edge labels in H,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} w(H) = \prod _{i \rightarrow j \in H}{\ell (i \rightarrow j)} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}w(H)=∏i→j∈Hℓ(i→j).We follow the standard convention that empty sums evaluate to zero and empty products evaluate to one. It follows that the weight of an edgeless graph is one. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H is a set of subgraphs of G, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w(\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}w(H) is the sum of the weights of the subgraphs in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}$$\end{document}H,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} w(\mathcal {H}) = \sum _{H \in \mathcal {H}}{w(H)} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}w(H)=∑H∈Hw(H).A subgraph F of G is a spanning forest of G if it contains every vertex in G (“spanning”), it contains no cycles of edges when edge directions are ignored (“forest”), and each vertex has at most one outgoing edge (which orients the forest). Since F has no cycles, it must have at least one vertex with no outgoing edges. Such vertices are the roots of F; let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varnothing \not = \mathcal {R}(F) \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}∅≠R(F)⊆V(G) denote the set of all roots of F. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}(F) = \{i\}$$\end{document}R(F)={i}, then F is a spanning tree rooted at i, as defined in the Introduction. Note that a spanning forest F is a disjoint union of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\#\mathcal {R}(F)$$\end{document}#R(F) trees, and must have exactly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N - \#\mathcal {R}(F)$$\end{document}N-#R(F) edges. We denote the set of all spanning forests of G rooted at A by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _A(G)$$\end{document}ΦA(G).

Given two vertices, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i, j \in \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}i,j∈V(G), we say that i leads to j, denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \leadsto j$$\end{document}i⇝j, if there is a directed path of edges from i to j: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i = i_1 \rightarrow \cdots \rightarrow i_k = j$$\end{document}i=i1→⋯→ik=j. There is always a trivial path from any vertex to itself, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \leadsto i$$\end{document}i⇝i. Furthermore, if F is a spanning forest of G, then any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}i∈V(G) has a unique directed path in F, with no repeated vertices, to exactly one root, namely the root of the tree containing i.

To accompany the vertex subsets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{V}_{\text {out}}(i)$$\end{document}Vout(i) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{V}_{\text {in}}(i)$$\end{document}Vin(i) coming from edges adjoining \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}i∈V(G), as defined above, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {out}}(i)$$\end{document}Vout(i) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (i)$$\end{document}Vin(i) the corresponding vertex subsets for directed paths that lead from i, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {out}}(i) = \{ j \in \mathcal {V}(G) : i \leadsto j \}$$\end{document}Vout(i)={j∈V(G):i⇝j}, and lead to i, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (i) = \{ j \in \mathcal {V}(G) : j \leadsto i \}$$\end{document}Vin(i)={j∈V(G):j⇝i}, respectively. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \not \in \textrm{V}_{\text {out}}(i)$$\end{document}i∉Vout(i) but \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {out}}(i) \not = \varnothing $$\end{document}i∈Vout(i)≠∅; similarly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \not \in \textrm{V}_{\text {in}}(i)$$\end{document}i∉Vin(i) but \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (i) \not = \varnothing $$\end{document}i∈Vin(i)≠∅. It is possible that one of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{V}_{\text {out}}(i)$$\end{document}Vout(i) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{V}_{\text {in}}(i)$$\end{document}Vin(i) is empty but not both, unless G consists solely of the vertex i.

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A, B \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}A,B⊆V(G) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\# A = \# B$$\end{document}#A=#B, define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{B \rightarrow A}(G) \subseteq \Phi _A(G)$$\end{document}ΦB→A(G)⊆ΦA(G) as the set of spanning forests of G rooted at A in which each vertex in B has a path to a distinct root in A. So, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\{1,2\} \rightarrow \{3,4\}}(G)$$\end{document}Φ{1,2}→{3,4}(G) is the set of all spanning forests of G rooted at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ 3,4 \right\} $$\end{document}3,4 in which either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \leadsto 3$$\end{document}1⇝3, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \leadsto 4$$\end{document}2⇝4 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \leadsto 4$$\end{document}1⇝4, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \leadsto 3$$\end{document}2⇝3. Since every root has a trivial path to itself, it follows that,6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi _{A \rightarrow A}(G) = \Phi _A(G) \qquad \text {for all }\, A \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΦA→A(G)=ΦA(G)for allA⊆V(G).If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = \{j\}$$\end{document}A={j}, then each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \Phi _A(G)$$\end{document}F∈ΦA(G) is a spanning tree and every vertex has a path to j. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\{i\} \rightarrow \{j\}}(G) = \Phi _{\{j\}}(G)$$\end{document}Φ{i}→{j}(G)=Φ{j}(G) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i, j \in \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}i,j∈V(G). A spanning forest \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \Phi _{B \rightarrow A}(G)$$\end{document}F∈ΦB→A(G) induces a bijection, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _F : B \rightarrow A$$\end{document}χF:B→A, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _F(i) \in A$$\end{document}χF(i)∈A is the unique root to which there is a path from i in F. Since a root always has a path to itself, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _F(i) = i$$\end{document}χF(i)=i for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in A \cap B$$\end{document}i∈A∩B. In particular, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = B$$\end{document}A=B, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _F : A \rightarrow A$$\end{document}χF:A→A is the identity for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \Phi _{A \rightarrow A}(G) = \Phi _A(G)$$\end{document}F∈ΦA→A(G)=ΦA(G) (Eqn. 6).

We say that i is strongly connected to j if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \leadsto j$$\end{document}i⇝j and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \leadsto i$$\end{document}j⇝i. Strong connectedness is an equivalence relation on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}V(G), and the corresponding equivalence classes are the strongly connected components (SCCs) of G. We denote the set of SCCs of G by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}(G)$$\end{document}C(G). Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C, D \in \mathcal {C}(G)$$\end{document}C,D∈C(G), we say that C precedes D, denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \prec D$$\end{document}C≺D, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \leadsto j$$\end{document}i⇝j for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in C$$\end{document}i∈C and some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \in D$$\end{document}j∈D. Since C and D are each strongly connected, this implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \leadsto j$$\end{document}i⇝j for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in C$$\end{document}i∈C and any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \in D$$\end{document}j∈D. In particular, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \prec D$$\end{document}C≺D and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D \prec C$$\end{document}D≺C, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C = D$$\end{document}C=D. Hence, the relation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \preceq D$$\end{document}C⪯D, defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C = D$$\end{document}C=D or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \prec D$$\end{document}C≺D, defines a partial order on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}(G)$$\end{document}C(G). The maximal elements under this partial order are the terminal SCCs of G, collectively denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}(G) \subseteq \mathcal {C}(G)$$\end{document}T(G)⊆C(G). Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{V}_{\text {out}}(i) = \varnothing $$\end{document}Vout(i)=∅ if, and only if, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{i\}$$\end{document}{i} is a singleton terminal SCC.

The terminal SCCs determine the minimal number of roots in a spanning forest. Suppose that G has T terminal SCCs, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1, \dotsc , C_T$$\end{document}C1,⋯,CT. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Q}(G) = C_1 \cup \cdots \cup C_T$$\end{document}Q(G)=C1∪⋯∪CT denote all the vertices within the terminal SCCs, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}(G) = \mathcal {V}(G) \setminus \mathcal {Q}(G)$$\end{document}N(G)=V(G)\Q(G) be all the vertices within the non-terminal SCCs. Since there can be no directed paths between terminal SCCs, any spanning forest F of G must have at least one root in each terminal SCC. Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\#\mathcal {R}(F) \ge T$$\end{document}#R(F)≥T. If G is strongly connected, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T = 1$$\end{document}T=1, then it is clear that the preceding inequality is sharp. If G is not strongly connected, then, because G is connected, it must be that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{N}(G) \not = \varnothing $$\end{document}N(G)≠∅. Each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {N}(G)$$\end{document}i∈N(G) must lead to some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \in \mathcal {Q}(G)$$\end{document}j∈Q(G) and, because G is connected, every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \in \mathcal {Q}(G)$$\end{document}j∈Q(G) must have some vertex in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}(G)$$\end{document}N(G) that leads to it. It follows that, provided \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}A⊆V(G) contains at least one vertex from each terminal SCC, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \cap C_k \not = \varnothing $$\end{document}A∩Ck≠∅ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le k \le T$$\end{document}1≤k≤T, then there is a spanning forest F with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}(F) = A$$\end{document}R(F)=A. In particular, there is always a spanning forest with exactly T roots, so that the inequality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\#\mathcal {R}(F) \ge T$$\end{document}#R(F)≥T is sharp.

The All-Minors Matrix-Tree theorem

The Matrix-Tree theorem (MTT) allows the steady-state probabilities of a graph to be expressed in terms of the edge labels, as shown by Eqns. 4 and 5. Here, we introduce the more general All-Minors Matrix-Tree theorem (AMMTT), which we use to express FPTs in the same way. This class of theorems reveals that Laplacian matrices, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)$$\end{document}L(G), have remarkable properties: the determinants of submatrices, or the minors, of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)$$\end{document}L(G) exhibit extensive sign cancellations and can be expressed in terms of the weights of spanning forests of G. We saw this for steady-state probabilities and spanning trees in Eqn. 4.

A bijection, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma : A \rightarrow A$$\end{document}σ:A→A, is a permutation on A, whose sign, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{sgn}\,}}(\sigma )$$\end{document}sgn(σ), is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(-1)^p$$\end{document}(-1)p, where p is the number of transpositions in any decomposition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ into a product of transpositions (Herstein 1975). Recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)_{[{\overline{B}},{\overline{A}}]}$$\end{document}L(G)[B¯,A¯] is the submatrix of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)$$\end{document}L(G) in which the rows indexed by B and the columns indexed by A have been removed. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\#A = \#B$$\end{document}#A=#B, then the determinant of such a submatrix is a minor of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)$$\end{document}L(G).

Theorem 1

(All-Minors Matrix-Tree theorem) Let G be a graph, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varnothing \not = A, B \subsetneq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}∅≠A,B⊊V(G) be proper, non-empty vertex subsets of the same size, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\# A = \# B = k$$\end{document}#A=#B=k. Write A and B in ascending order as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = \left\{ a_1< \cdots < a_k \right\} $$\end{document}A=a1<⋯<ak and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B = \left\{ b_1< \cdots < b_k \right\} $$\end{document}B=b1<⋯<bk and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta : A \rightarrow B$$\end{document}η:A→B be the bijection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (a_i) = b_i$$\end{document}η(ai)=bi for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i = 1, \dotsc , k$$\end{document}i=1,⋯,k. Then,7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det {\mathcal {L}(G)_{[{\overline{B}},{\overline{A}}]}} = (-1)^{N - k + \sum _{a \in A}{a} + \sum _{b \in B}{b}} \sum _{F \in \Phi _{B \rightarrow A}(G)}{{{\,\textrm{sgn}\,}}(\chi _F \circ \eta ) \, w(F)} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}detL(G)[B¯,A¯]=(-1)N-k+∑a∈Aa+∑b∈Bb∑F∈ΦB→A(G)sgn(χF∘η)w(F),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _F : B \rightarrow A$$\end{document}χF:B→A is the bijection defined after Eqn. 6.

If we take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = \left\{ i \right\} $$\end{document}A=i and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B = \left\{ j \right\} $$\end{document}B=j, the AMMTT reduces to the MTT (Mirzaev and Gunawardena 2013), from which Eqn. 4 is derived. The MTT has a long history that goes back to the 19th century work of Kirchhoff—see Mirzaev and Gunawardena (2013) for details—but the first report of the AMMTT is due to Fiedler and Sedláček (1958) in the Czech mathematical literature; subsequent proofs were given by Chaiken (1982) and Moon (1994).Fig. 2(A) The seven spanning forests in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\{2,4\} \rightarrow \{1,4\}}(K)$$\end{document}Φ{2,4}→{1,4}(K), where K is the example graph in Fig. 1A, with their corresponding weights. Roots are shown in red. (B) Schematic of the maps between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,4\}$$\end{document}{1,4} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{2,4\}$$\end{document}{2,4} for each forest F in panel A. In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _F$$\end{document}χF is the same for all F and is the inverse of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η

(A) The seven spanning forests in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\{2,4\} \rightarrow \{1,4\}}(K)$$\end{document}Φ{2,4}→{1,4}(K), where K is the example graph in Fig. 1A, with their corresponding weights. Roots are shown in red. (B) Schematic of the maps between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,4\}$$\end{document}{1,4} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{2,4\}$$\end{document}{2,4} for each forest F in panel A. In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _F$$\end{document}χF is the same for all F and is the inverse of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η

As an example of Theorem 1 in action, consider the graph, K, in Fig. 1A, whose Laplacian matrix, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(K)$$\end{document}L(K), is also shown there. The submatrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(K)_{[\overline{\{2,4\}},\overline{\{1,4\}}]}$$\end{document}L(K)[{2,4}¯,{1,4}¯] is given by8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left[ \begin{array}{ccc} k_2 & 0 & k_{11} \\ k_3 & -k_5 - k_6 - k_7 & 0 \\ k_4 & k_7 & -k_{11} \\ \end{array} \right] \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}k20k11k3-k5-k6-k70k4k7-k11.According to Theorem 1, the minor, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det {\mathcal {L}(K)_{[\overline{\{2,4\}}, \overline{\{1,4\}}]}}$$\end{document}detL(K)[{2,4}¯,{1,4}¯], may be calculated in terms of the spanning forests in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\{2,4\} \rightarrow \{1,4\}}(K)$$\end{document}Φ{2,4}→{1,4}(K), which are enumerated in Fig. 2A. Since vertex 4 is common to both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1,4\}$$\end{document}{1,4} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{2,4\}$$\end{document}{2,4}, the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _F: \{2,4\} \rightarrow \{1,4\}$$\end{document}χF:{2,4}→{1,4} is the same for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \Phi _{\{2,4\} \rightarrow \{1,4\}}(K)$$\end{document}F∈Φ{2,4}→{1,4}(K), and this map is also the inverse of the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta : \{1,4\} \rightarrow \{2,4\}$$\end{document}η:{1,4}→{2,4} defined in Theorem 1 (Fig. 2B). It follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{sgn}\,}}{(\chi _F \circ \eta )} = 1$$\end{document}sgn(χF∘η)=1 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \Phi _{\{2,4\} \rightarrow \{1,4\}}(K)$$\end{document}F∈Φ{2,4}→{1,4}(K). Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N - k = 5 - 2 = 3$$\end{document}N-k=5-2=3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1 + 4) + (2 + 4) = 11$$\end{document}(1+4)+(2+4)=11 combine to yield a sign of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(-1)^{14} = 1$$\end{document}(-1)14=1. It follows from Theorem 1 that,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det {\mathcal {L}(K)_{[\overline{\{2,4\}}, \overline{\{1,4\}}]}} = k_{11} \left( k_2 k_5 + k_2 k_7 + k_4 k_5 + k_4 k_7 + k_3 k_7 + k_2 k_6 + k_4 k_6 \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}detL(K)[{2,4}¯,{1,4}¯]=k11k2k5+k2k7+k4k5+k4k7+k3k7+k2k6+k4k6,which may easily be confirmed from Eqn. 8.

As a further example of how Theorem 1 works, consider the extreme case in which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = \overline{\{i\}}$$\end{document}A={i}¯ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B = \overline{\{j\}}$$\end{document}B={j}¯ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \not = j$$\end{document}i≠j, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k = N-1$$\end{document}k=N-1. Then,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}(G)_{[{\overline{B}},{\overline{A}}]} = \mathcal {L}(G)_{j,i}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(G)[B¯,A¯]=L(G)j,i,so that (Eqn. 2),9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \det {\mathcal {L}(G)_{[{\overline{B}},{\overline{A}}]}} = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \ell (i \rightarrow j) & \text{ if }\,\, i \rightarrow j \\ 0 & \text{ if }\,\, i \not \rightarrow j. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}detL(G)[B¯,A¯]=ℓ(i→j)ifi→j0ifi↛j.To see what Theorem 1 says in this context, note that a spanning forest in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{B \rightarrow A}(G)$$\end{document}ΦB→A(G) must contain only a single edge, which must evidently be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \rightarrow j$$\end{document}i→j, should the edge exist. Hence, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \not \rightarrow j$$\end{document}i↛j, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{B \rightarrow A}(G) = \varnothing $$\end{document}ΦB→A(G)=∅ and Theorem 1 correctly gives the second case in Eqn. 9. If, however, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \rightarrow j$$\end{document}i→j does exist in G, then Theorem 1 says that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det {\mathcal {L}(G)_{[{\overline{B}},{\overline{A}}]}}$$\end{document}detL(G)[B¯,A¯] is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( -1 \right) ^{N - (N-1) + \sum _{a \in A}{a} + \sum _{b \in B}{b}} {{\,\textrm{sgn}\,}}{\left( \chi _F \circ \eta \right) } \, \ell (i \rightarrow j). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-1N-(N-1)+∑a∈Aa+∑b∈BbsgnχF∘ηℓ(i→j).We now examine how the two signs work out. First, note that the parity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 + \sum _{a \in A} a + \sum _{b \in B} b = 1 + 2 \left( 1 + \cdots + N \right) - i - j$$\end{document}1+∑a∈Aa+∑b∈Bb=1+21+⋯+N-i-j is the same as that of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 - i - j$$\end{document}1-i-j. As for the sign of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _F \circ \eta $$\end{document}χF∘η, assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i < j$$\end{document}i<j and let us write A and B out as a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \times (N-1)$$\end{document}2×(N-1) array of entries,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A:1,⋯,i-1,i_,i+1,⋯,j-1,j,j+1,⋯,NB:1,⋯,i-1,i,i+1,⋯,j-1,j_,j+1,⋯,N,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts}
