\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{j}$$\end{document}j_ signify their absence from the array. It is clear that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _F$$\end{document}χF takes i to j and is the identity elsewhere, while from the array above, we see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η takes m to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m-1$$\end{document}m-1 if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i < m \le j$$\end{document}i<m≤j and is the identity elsewhere. It follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _F \circ \eta $$\end{document}χF∘η is the permutation,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \begin{array}{ccccccccccc} 1 & \cdots & i-1 & i+1 & i+2 & \cdots & j-1 & j & j+1 & \cdots & N \\ 1 & \cdots & i-1 & j & i+1 & \cdots & j-2 & j-1 & j+1 & \cdots & N \end{array} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1⋯i-1i+1i+2⋯j-1jj+1⋯N1⋯i-1ji+1⋯j-2j-1j+1⋯N,which can be written in cycle notation as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(j, j-1, j-2, \dotsc , i+1)$$\end{document}(j,j-1,j-2,⋯,i+1). This cycle has length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j - i$$\end{document}j-i, and therefore can be written as a product of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j - i - 1$$\end{document}j-i-1 transpositions, so that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{sgn}\,}}{(\chi _F \circ \eta )} = \left( -1 \right) ^{j-i-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}sgn(χF∘η)=-1j-i-1.Accordingly, the total sign is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( -1 \right) ^{1-i-j+j-i-1} = \left( -1 \right) ^{-2i} = 1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-11-i-j+j-i-1=-1-2i=1.Theorem 1 therefore tells us that, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \rightarrow j$$\end{document}i→j does exist in G, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det {\mathcal {L}(G)_{[{\overline{B}},{\overline{A}}]}} = \ell (i \rightarrow j)$$\end{document}detL(G)[B¯,A¯]=ℓ(i→j), which agrees with the first case in Eqn. 9. We hope these examples illustrate the workings of Theorem 1.

Preliminary results

In this section, we state and prove some basic results that follow from Theorem 1, upon which we will rely throughout the paper.

We need some machinery to accomplish this. It will be convenient to define, for any finite, ordered set, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S = \left\{ s_1< \cdots < s_k \right\} $$\end{document}S=s1<⋯<sk, the ordering bijection,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta _S : \left\{ 1, \dotsc , k \right\} \rightarrow S, \qquad \theta _S(i) = s_i \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θS:1,⋯,k→S,θS(i)=si.This bijection is helpful to keep track of the rows and columns of submatrices, which acquire a different indexing to those of the original matrix. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}A⊆V(G) be a subset with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\# A = k$$\end{document}#A=k, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i, j \in \left\{ 1, \dotsc , k \right\} $$\end{document}i,j∈1,⋯,k. Then the (i, j)-th entry of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \times k$$\end{document}k×k submatrix, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)_{[A, A]}$$\end{document}L(G)[A,A], corresponds to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\theta _A(i), \theta _A(j))$$\end{document}(θA(i),θA(j))-th entry of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)$$\end{document}L(G),10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \mathcal {L}(G)_{[A, A]} \right) _{i,j} = \mathcal {L}(G)_{\theta _A(i), \theta _A(j)} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(G)[A,A]i,j=L(G)θA(i),θA(j).Alternatively, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i, j \in A$$\end{document}i,j∈A, then we can invert the correspondence in Eqn. 10 to see that,11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}(G)_{i,j} = \left( \mathcal {L}(G)_{[A, A]} \right) _{\theta _A^{-1}(i), \theta _A^{-1}(j)} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(G)i,j=L(G)[A,A]θA-1(i),θA-1(j).We now consider what happens when we re-index the vertices of G by a permutation, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma : \mathcal {V}(G) \rightarrow \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}σ:V(G)→V(G). What this means is that the edge, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \rightarrow _G i$$\end{document}j→Gi, becomes the edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (j) \rightarrow _{G^{\sigma }} \sigma (i)$$\end{document}σ(j)→Gσσ(i) and retains the same label, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell (j \rightarrow _G i) = \ell (\sigma (j) \rightarrow _{G^{\sigma }} \sigma (i))$$\end{document}ℓ(j→Gi)=ℓ(σ(j)→Gσσ(i)). It then follows from Eqn. 2 that,12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}(G)_{i,j} = \mathcal {L}(G^\sigma )_{\sigma (i), \sigma (j)} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(G)i,j=L(Gσ)σ(i),σ(j),so that the rows and columns of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)$$\end{document}L(G) are permuted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ to form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G^{\sigma })$$\end{document}L(Gσ). If we have the same subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}A⊆V(G) as above, it will give rise under the permutation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ to the subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (A) \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G^{\sigma })$$\end{document}σ(A)⊆V(Gσ). If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,j \in \sigma (A)$$\end{document}i,j∈σ(A), then applying Eqn. 11 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^{\sigma }$$\end{document}Gσ, we see that,13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}(G^\sigma )_{i,j} = \left( \mathcal {L}(G^\sigma )_{[\sigma (A), \sigma (A)]} \right) _{\theta _{\sigma (A)}^{-1}(i), \theta _{\sigma (A)}^{-1}(j)} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(Gσ)i,j=L(Gσ)[σ(A),σ(A)]θσ(A)-1(i),θσ(A)-1(j).Now let us return to choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,j \in \{1, \dotsc , k\}$$\end{document}i,j∈{1,⋯,k} and consider the sequence of bijections,which amount to a form of conjugation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _A$$\end{document}θA. Let us call the composition of these bijections \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _{\sigma , A}: \{1, \dotsc , k\} \rightarrow \{1, \dotsc , k\}$$\end{document}τσ,A:{1,⋯,k}→{1,⋯,k},14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tau _{\sigma ,A} = \theta _{\sigma (A)}^{-1}\left( \sigma \left( \theta _A \right) \right) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}τσ,A=θσ(A)-1σθA.Applying Eqns. 10, 12 and 13 in sequence, we then see that,15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \left( \mathcal {L}(G)_{[A, A]} \right) _{i,j}&= \mathcal {L}(G)_{\theta _A(i), \theta _A(j)} \\&= \mathcal {L}(G^{\sigma })_{\sigma (\theta _A(i)), \sigma (\theta _A(j))} \\&= \left( \mathcal {L}(G^\sigma )_{[\sigma (A), \sigma (A)]} \right) _{\tau _{\sigma ,A}(i), \tau _{\sigma ,A}(j)} \,. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(G)[A,A]i,j=L(G)θA(i),θA(j)=L(Gσ)σ(θA(i)),σ(θA(j))=L(Gσ)[σ(A),σ(A)]τσ,A(i),τσ,A(j).The impact of the permutation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ on spanning forests is less intricate. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}A⊆V(G) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \Phi _A(G)$$\end{document}F∈ΦA(G), then applying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ to the vertices of F yields a re-indexed forest, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^*(F) \in \Phi _{\sigma (A)}(G^\sigma )$$\end{document}σ∗(F)∈Φσ(A)(Gσ), with the same weight, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w(\sigma ^*(F)) = w(F)$$\end{document}w(σ∗(F))=w(F). If we have a second subset, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}B⊆V(G), with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\# B = \# A$$\end{document}#B=#A, then the map, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^* : \Phi _A(G) \rightarrow \Phi _{\sigma (A)}(G^\sigma )$$\end{document}σ∗:ΦA(G)→Φσ(A)(Gσ), which is evidently a bijection, restricts to a bijection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^* : \Phi _{B \rightarrow A}(G) \rightarrow \Phi _{\sigma (B) \rightarrow \sigma (A)}(G^\sigma )$$\end{document}σ∗:ΦB→A(G)→Φσ(B)→σ(A)(Gσ). It follows that,16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} w(\Phi _{B \rightarrow A}(G)) = w(\Phi _{\sigma (B) \rightarrow \sigma (A)}(G^\sigma )) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}w(ΦB→A(G))=w(Φσ(B)→σ(A)(Gσ)).Finally, we review some linear algebra. To derive the main results in this paper, we shall primarily invoke Theorem 1 in combination with Cramer’s rule. Recall that, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}$$\end{document}M is an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \times N$$\end{document}N×N matrix, then the adjugate of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}$$\end{document}M, which we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{adj}\,}}\textbf{M}$$\end{document}adjM, is the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \times N$$\end{document}N×N matrix with entries,17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} ({{\text {adj}\,}} {\textbf {M}})_{i,j} = (-1)^{i+j} \det {{\textbf {M}}_{[\overline{\{j\}}, \overline{\{i\}}]}} \,. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(adjM)i,j=(-1)i+jdetM[{j}¯,{i}¯].Cramer’s rule then states that,18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} {\textbf {M}}\, ({{\text {adj}\,}} {\textbf {M}}) = ({{\text {adj}\,}} {\textbf {M}}) \, {\textbf {M}}= (\det {\textbf {M}}) \, {\textbf {I}}\,. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(adjM)=(adjM)M=(detM)I.It follows that, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\det \textbf{M}\ne 0$$\end{document}detM≠0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {M}}^{-1} = ({{\text {adj}\,}} {\textbf {M}}) / (\det {\textbf {M}})$$\end{document}M-1=(adjM)/(detM).

This preparation leads us to one of the central technical results of the paper.

Proposition 2

Let G be a graph and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varnothing \ne A \subsetneq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}∅≠A⊊V(G) be a subset of k vertices, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< k < N$$\end{document}0<k<N. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)_{[{\overline{A}},{\overline{A}}]}$$\end{document}L(G)[A¯,A¯] is invertible if, and only if, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _A(G)$$\end{document}ΦA(G) is non-empty. Furthermore, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)_{[{\overline{A}},{\overline{A}}]}$$\end{document}L(G)[A¯,A¯] is invertible, the inverse is given by,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \mathcal {L}(G)_{[{\overline{A}},{\overline{A}}]} \right) ^{-1}_{i,j} = -\frac{w(\Phi _{A \cup \{\theta _{{\overline{A}}}(j)\} \rightarrow A \cup \{\theta _{{\overline{A}}}(i)\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _A(G))} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(G)[A¯,A¯]i,j-1=-w(ΦA∪{θA¯(j)}→A∪{θA¯(i)}(G))w(ΦA(G)),for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i, j \in \left\{ 1, \dotsc , N-k \right\} $$\end{document}i,j∈1,⋯,N-k.

Proof

The first statement follows by setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = B$$\end{document}A=B in Eqn. 7 and recalling Eqn. 6. To prove the second statement, we first show that it holds when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = \left\{ N-k+1, \dotsc , N \right\} $$\end{document}A=N-k+1,⋯,N, in which case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{{\overline{A}}}(i) = i$$\end{document}θA¯(i)=i for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i = 1, \dotsc , N-k$$\end{document}i=1,⋯,N-k. The following elementary result on spanning forests will also be helpful in what follows. Choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}A⊆V(G) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i, j \in {\overline{A}}$$\end{document}i,j∈A¯ such that the set, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{A \cup \{i\} \rightarrow A \cup \{j\}}(G)$$\end{document}ΦA∪{i}→A∪{j}(G), of spanning forests rooted at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \cup \{j\}$$\end{document}A∪{j} with a path from i to j is non-empty, and let F be such a spanning forest. If G contains an edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \rightarrow z$$\end{document}j→z for some other root \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in A$$\end{document}z∈A, then adjoining this edge to F yields a spanning forest that is rooted at A and contains a path from i to z. That is, the new spanning forest lies in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{(A \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i\} \rightarrow A}(G)$$\end{document}Φ(A\{z})∪{i}→A(G). Conversely, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in \Phi _{(A \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i\} \rightarrow A}(G)$$\end{document}F∈Φ(A\{z})∪{i}→A(G), then F must contain a path from i to z. Since i is itself not a root, this path must possess at least one edge; if the last edge on this path is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \rightarrow z$$\end{document}j→z, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \not \in A$$\end{document}j∉A and omitting this edge creates a forest in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{A \cup \{i\} \rightarrow A \cup \{j\}}(G)$$\end{document}ΦA∪{i}→A∪{j}(G). These procedures are evidently inverse to each other.

Lemma 3

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}A⊆V(G), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in {\overline{A}}$$\end{document}i∈A¯ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in A$$\end{document}z∈A, the map,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bigcup _{j \in \textrm{V}_{\text {in}}(z) \setminus A}{\Phi _{A \cup \{i\} \rightarrow A \cup \{j\}}(G)} \rightarrow \Phi _{(A \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i\} \rightarrow A}(G) \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⋃j∈Vin(z)\AΦA∪{i}→A∪{j}(G)→Φ(A\{z})∪{i}→A(G),that adjoins the edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \rightarrow z$$\end{document}j→z is a bijection.

It follows immediately from Lemma 3 that, under the same assumptions,24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{j \in \textrm{V}_{\text {in}}(z) \setminus A}w(\Phi _{A \cup \{i\} \rightarrow A \cup \{j\}}(G)) \, \ell (j \rightarrow z) = w(\Phi _{(A \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i\} \rightarrow A}(G)) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑j∈Vin(z)\Aw(ΦA∪{i}→A∪{j}(G))ℓ(j→z)=w(Φ(A\{z})∪{i}→A(G)).

FPTs in graphs with singleton terminal SCCs

With the above preliminaries in hand, we can embark on calculating FPTs. This will require several steps. We will derive the basic time-evolution equation, use the Laplace transform to calculate moment-like quantities and then obtain formulas for the splitting probabilities and the moments of the conditional FPT distribution.

Definitions and notation

To simplify the argument, we will assume that each terminal SCC is a singleton, which we will refer to as a terminal vertex. Let Z denote the set of terminal vertices. More general situations can be reduced to this special case, as we will explain subsequently.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(\cdot )$$\end{document}X(·) be the Markov process associated with G, and choose some starting vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}i∈V(G) and some terminal vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in Z$$\end{document}z∈Z. We will argue somewhat intuitively here, to avoid getting into measure-theoretic complexities; see Anderson (1991); Norris (1997); Serfozo (2009) for the rigorous underpinnings. We consider stochastically generating a large finite ensemble, E, of unbounded trajectories of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(\cdot )$$\end{document}X(·) each starting from i, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(0) = i$$\end{document}X(0)=i. With probability one, each trajectory will reach some terminal vertex in Z and stay there from then on. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{z,t} \subseteq E$$\end{document}Az,t⊆E be the subset of those trajectories that have reached z by time t. The probability of reaching z from i by time t is then given by,25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} p_{i,z}(t) = \Pr \left[ X(t') = z\,\, \text{ for } \text{ some } \,\,0 \le t' \le t \mid X(0) = i\right] \approx \frac{\# A_{z,t}}{\# E} \,, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pi,z(t)=PrX(t′)=zforsome0≤t′≤t∣X(0)=i≈#Az,t#E,where the approximation becomes exact in the limit of an infinite ensemble. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{z,t}$$\end{document}Az,t clearly enlarges with t, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}pi,z(t) must be non-decreasing, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(t_1) \le p_{i,z}(t_2)$$\end{document}pi,z(t1)≤pi,z(t2) if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_1 \le t_2$$\end{document}t1≤t2, so that it resembles a cumulative distribution function. However, it is not a valid cumulative distribution function over t because it is not correctly normalised: the quantity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{t \rightarrow \infty }p_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}limt→∞pi,z(t), which we denote for convenience as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(\infty )$$\end{document}pi,z(∞), is not 1 but, rather, the splitting probability, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{i,z}$$\end{document}πi,z, of eventually reaching z from i. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_z \subseteq E$$\end{document}Bz⊆E be the subset of trajectories that eventually reach z. Then,26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \pi _{i,z} = \Pr \left[ X(t) = z \,\,\text{ for } \text{ some }\,\, t \ge 0 \mid X(0) = i \right] \approx \frac{\# B_z}{\# E} \,. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}πi,z=PrX(t)=zforsomet≥0∣X(0)=i≈#Bz#E.For sufficiently large t, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{z,t} = B_z$$\end{document}Az,t=Bz, so it follows from Eqn. 25 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(\infty ) = \pi _{i,z}$$\end{document}pi,z(∞)=πi,z. This splitting probability is one of the quantities that we want to calculate.

The other quantities of interest arise from the conditional FPT distribution, which we can approach through the probability, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{c,i,z}(t)$$\end{document}pc,i,z(t), of reaching z from i by time t, conditioned on reaching z eventually. For this probability to be well-defined, it is necessary that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z)$$\end{document}i∈Vin(z), so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_z \not = \varnothing $$\end{document}Bz≠∅. With that proviso,27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} p_{c,i,z}(t)&= \Pr [ X(t') = z \,\,\text{ for } \text{ some }\,\, 0 \le t' \le t \,\,\mid \\ &\qquad \qquad X(0) = i \,\,\text{ and }\,\, X(u) = z\,\, \text{ for } \text{ some }\,\, u > 0] \\ &\approx \frac{\# A_{z,t}}{\# B_z} \,. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pc,i,z(t)=Pr[X(t′)=zforsome0≤t′≤t∣X(0)=iandX(u)=zforsomeu>0]≈#Az,t#Bz.It follows from Eqns. 26 and 27 that, as long as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z)$$\end{document}i∈Vin(z),28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_{c,i,z}(t) = \frac{p_{i,z}(t)}{\pi _{i,z}} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pc,i,z(t)=pi,z(t)πi,z.In contrast to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}pi,z(t), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{c,i,z}(t)$$\end{document}pc,i,z(t) is a cumulative distribution function that is correctly normalised with respect to t, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{c,i,z}(\infty ) = 1$$\end{document}pc,i,z(∞)=1. Its time derivative, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$dp_{c,i,z}(t) / dt$$\end{document}dpc,i,z(t)/dt, gives the probability density of the conditional FPT distribution over t. We want to calculate the moments of this distribution.

We also consider the probability to reach any terminal vertex in Z. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_t \subseteq E$$\end{document}At⊆E be those trajectories that have reached Z by time t. Then we can define,29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} p_{i,Z}(t) = \Pr \left[ X(t') \in Z\,\, \text{ for } \text{ some }\,\, 0 \le t' \le t \mid X(0) = i\right] \approx \frac{\# A_t}{\# E} \,. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pi,Z(t)=PrX(t′)∈Zforsome0≤t′≤t∣X(0)=i≈#At#E.Evidently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,Z}(\infty ) = 1$$\end{document}pi,Z(∞)=1, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,Z}(t)$$\end{document}pi,Z(t) is also a bona fide cumulative distribution function, whose corresponding probability density is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$dp_{i,Z}(t) / dt$$\end{document}dpi,Z(t)/dt. It is also clear that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_t = \bigcup _{z \in Z}A_{z,t}$$\end{document}At=⋃z∈ZAz,t and that this union is disjoint, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\# A_t = \sum _{z \in Z} \# A_{z,t}$$\end{document}#At=∑z∈Z#Az,t. It follows from Eqns. 26 and 27 that,30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_{i,Z}(t) = \sum _{z \in Z : i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z)} p_{c,i,z}(t) \, \pi _{i,z} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pi,Z(t)=∑z∈Z:i∈Vin(z)pc,i,z(t)πi,z.Instead of working directly with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{c,i,z}(t)$$\end{document}pc,i,z(t) to calculate FPTs, it is easier to work with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}pi,z(t), for which we can find a system of differential equations for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z)$$\end{document}i∈Vin(z). From there, via the Laplace transform, we can determine the splitting probabilities, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{i,z}$$\end{document}πi,z, the moments of the conditional FPT to each terminal vertex, and the moments of the FPT to any terminal vertex.

We need some further notation before embarking on this calculation. Let G be a graph with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \ge 1$$\end{document}T≥1 terminal SCCs. We can assume, without loss of generality, that the vertices are indexed so that the terminal SCCs are as follows,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {T}(G) = \left\{ \left\{ N-T+1 \right\} , \dotsc , \left\{ N \right\} \right\} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T(G)=N-T+1,⋯,N,so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z = \left\{ N-T+1, \dotsc , N \right\} $$\end{document}Z=N-T+1,⋯,N. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}i∈V(G), then it must have a path to some terminal vertex. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}(i) \subseteq Z$$\end{document}Z(i)⊆Z denote the subset of those terminal vertices,31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {Z}(i) = \mathcal {V}_{\text {out}}(i) \cap Z \not = \varnothing \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Z(i)=Vout(i)∩Z≠∅.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}(i) = \{i\}$$\end{document}Z(i)={i} if, and only if, i is itself terminal.

Let us now choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in Z$$\end{document}z∈Z. It will be held fixed for much of the argument that follows. The vertices that lead to z, excluding z itself, which form the subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z) \setminus \{z\}$$\end{document}Vin(z)\{z}, must all be non-terminal. We can, again without loss of generality, index these non-terminal vertices as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1, \dotsc , N_z$$\end{document}1,⋯,Nz, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_z \le N - T$$\end{document}Nz≤N-T. Note that this indexing depends on the choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in Z$$\end{document}z∈Z. It follows that,32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z) = \left\{ 1, \dotsc , N_z, z \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Vin(z)=1,⋯,Nz,z.It will be helpful to let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)$$\end{document}Vin-(z) denote just the non-terminal vertices in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z)$$\end{document}Vin(z), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z) = \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z) \setminus \{z\}$$\end{document}Vin-(z)=Vin(z)\{z}. Let us also choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z)$$\end{document}i∈Vin(z), so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \mathcal {Z}(i)$$\end{document}z∈Z(i). The vertex i will also be held fixed for much of the following argument.

The basic time-evolution equation

With that preparation, we can now derive a system of differential equations for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}pi,z(t). First note that, because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(\cdot )$$\end{document}X(·) is time-homogeneous, if we shift the time axis by h, so that trajectories start at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(h) = i$$\end{document}X(h)=i, then the probability of reaching z by time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t + h$$\end{document}t+h is also given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}pi,z(t). Recall from the elementary properties of Markov processes that the probability that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(\cdot )$$\end{document}X(·) remains at i up to time t is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-\lambda _i t}$$\end{document}e-λit, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i$$\end{document}λi is the total exit rate from i,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _i = \sum _{j \in \textrm{V}_{\text {out}}(i)}\ell (i \rightarrow j) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λi=∑j∈Vout(i)ℓ(i→j).The probability that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(\cdot )$$\end{document}X(·) has left i by time t is then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 - e^{-\lambda _i t}$$\end{document}1-e-λit. If we choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< h < t$$\end{document}0<h<t sufficiently small, then the probability that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(\cdot )$$\end{document}X(·) has taken one transition from i by time h is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _i h$$\end{document}λih, to first order in h. The probability that it has taken k transitions by time h can then be no less than order k in h. Accordingly, to first order in h, we may assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(\cdot )$$\end{document}X(·) has taken no more than one transition from i. There are then two mutually exclusive possibilities. First, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(\cdot )$$\end{document}X(·) remains at i up to time h, with probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-\lambda _i h}$$\end{document}e-λih, and then reaches z within the remaining time from h to t. Because of the time-homogeneity mentioned above, the latter probability is just \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(t-h)$$\end{document}pi,z(t-h). Second, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(\cdot )$$\end{document}X(·) leaves i by time h, which it does with probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 - e^{-\lambda _i h}$$\end{document}1-e-λih, and takes the edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \rightarrow j$$\end{document}i→j, which it does with probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell (i \rightarrow j)/\lambda _i$$\end{document}ℓ(i→j)/λi, and then reaches z from j within the remaining time from h to t, which, as before, occurs with probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{j,z}(t-h)$$\end{document}pj,z(t-h). Putting these parts together, we see that,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p_{i,z}(t) = e^{-\lambda _i h} \, p_{i,z}(t-h) + (1 - e^{-\lambda _i h}) \sum _{j \in \textrm{V}_{\text {out}}(i)}\left( \frac{\ell (i \rightarrow j)}{\lambda _i} \right) p_{j,z}(t - h) + \textrm{o}(h) \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pi,z(t)=e-λihpi,z(t-h)+(1-e-λih)∑j∈Vout(i)ℓ(i→j)λipj,z(t-h)+o(h),where the “little o” notation, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{o}(h)$$\end{document}o(h), denotes some function f(h) which is of order higher than 1 in h, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{h \rightarrow 0}f(h)/h = 0$$\end{document}limh→0f(h)/h=0. If we subtract \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(t - h)$$\end{document}pi,z(t-h) from both sides, divide through by h, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}h→0, then, keeping in mind that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{h \rightarrow 0}(1 - e^{-\lambda _i h})/h = \lambda _i$$\end{document}limh→0(1-e-λih)/h=λi, we obtain,33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt} p_{i,z}(t) = \sum _{j \in \textrm{V}_{\text {out}}(i)}\ell (i \rightarrow j) \, p_{j,z}(t) - \lambda _i \, p_{i,z}(t) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtpi,z(t)=∑j∈Vout(i)ℓ(i→j)pj,z(t)-λipi,z(t).We could use Eqn. 28 to rewrite Eqn. 33 as a system of equations for the conditional probabilities, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{c,i,z}(t)$$\end{document}pc,i,z(t), but this would also involve the splitting probabilities, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{i,z}$$\end{document}πi,z, which are themselves unknown. This illustrates why it is preferable to work with the system of equations for the unconditional probabilities, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}pi,z(t), in Eqn. 33.

We can rewrite Eqn. 33 in terms of the underlying graph G by defining the vector of probabilities to reach z by time t, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}_z(t) = (p_{1,z}(t), \dotsc , p_{N_z,z}(t))^\textrm{T}\in \mathbb {R}^{N_z}$$\end{document}pz(t)=(p1,z(t),⋯,pNz,z(t))T∈RNz, where the indices run over the non-terminal vertices of G that lead to z, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)$$\end{document}i∈Vin-(z). (Note that, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i = z$$\end{document}i=z, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(t) = p_{z,z}(t) = 1$$\end{document}pi,z(t)=pz,z(t)=1 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \ge 0$$\end{document}t≥0.) Now, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{p}_z^+(t) \in \mathbb {R}^{N_z + 1}$$\end{document}pz+(t)∈RNz+1 denote the augmented vector,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{p}_z^+(t) = (p_{1,z}(t), \dotsc , p_{N_z,z}(t), p_{z,z}(t))^\textrm{T}= (p_{1,z}(t), \dotsc , p_{N_z,z}(t), 1)^\textrm{T}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}pz+(t)=(p1,z(t),⋯,pNz,z(t),pz,z(t))T=(p1,z(t),⋯,pNz,z(t),1)T.Then, rewriting Eqn. 33, we see that,34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt} \textbf{p}_z(t) = \left( \mathcal {L}(G)^\textrm{T}\right) _{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z)]} \textbf{p}_z^{+}(t) \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtpz(t)=L(G)T[Vin-(z),Vin(z)]pz+(t),where the submatrix of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathcal {L}}}(G)^\textrm{T}$$\end{document}L(G)T has size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_z \times (N_z + 1)$$\end{document}Nz×(Nz+1). Eqn. 34 is the adjoint master equation of the Markov process (Iyer-Biswas et al. 2016; van Kampen 2007), so named because its operator is the transpose to that in the master equation (Eqn. 1).

The Laplace transform

The calculation of moments is most readily done by using the Laplace transform of the probability density. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}ui,z(t) be the time derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}pi,z(t),35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_{i,z}(t) = \frac{dp_{i,z}(t)}{dt} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ui,z(t)=dpi,z(t)dt.As noted above, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}ui,z(t) is generally not the density of a probability distribution over t because it is not correctly normalised, but it can serve the same purpose here. Differentiating Eqn. 34 with respect to t yields a similar adjoint time-evolution equation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{u}_z(t) = \left( u_{1,z}(t), \dotsc , u_{N_z,z}(t) \right) ^\textrm{T}$$\end{document}uz(t)=u1,z(t),⋯,uNz,z(t)T,36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt} \textbf{u}_z(t) = \left( \mathcal {L}(G)^\textrm{T}\right) _{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \textbf{u}_z(t) \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtuz(t)=L(G)T[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]uz(t),where we have used the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{z,z}(t) = 0$$\end{document}uz,z(t)=0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \ge 0$$\end{document}t≥0 to drop the final column in the right-hand matrix, which now has size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_z \times N_z$$\end{document}Nz×Nz.

Now, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{u}_{i,z}(s) = \mathscr {L}{\left\{ u_{i,z}(t) \right\} }$$\end{document}u~i,z(s)=Lui,z(t) denote the Laplace transform of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}ui,z(t),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widetilde{u}_{i,z}(s) = \mathscr {L}{\left\{ u_{i,z}(t) \right\} } = \int _0^\infty {e^{-st} \, u_{i,z}(t) \, dt} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u~i,z(s)=Lui,z(t)=∫0∞e-stui,z(t)dt.Applying the Laplace transform to both sides of Eqn. 36 and using the well-known property that it converts differentiation by t into multiplication by s, we get a linear algebraic equation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\textbf{u}}_z(s) = \left( \widetilde{u}_{1,z}(s), \dotsc , \widetilde{u}_{N_z,z}(s) \right) ^\textrm{T}$$\end{document}u~z(s)=u~1,z(s),⋯,u~Nz,z(s)T,37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} s \widetilde{\textbf{u}}_z(s) - \textbf{u}_z(0) = \left( \mathcal {L}(G)^\textrm{T}\right) _{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \widetilde{\textbf{u}}_z(s) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}su~z(s)-uz(0)=L(G)T[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]u~z(s).To determine the initial condition vector, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{u}_z(0)$$\end{document}uz(0), note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{i,z}(0) = 0$$\end{document}pi,z(0)=0 if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \not = z$$\end{document}i≠z, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{z,z}(0) = 1$$\end{document}pz,z(0)=1. Substituting into Eqn. 33 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t = 0$$\end{document}t=0, we obtain,38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_{i,z}(0) = \left. \frac{dp_{i,z}(t)}{dt} \right| _{t = 0} = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \ell (i \rightarrow z) & \text {if}\, i \rightarrow z \\ 0 & \text {otherwise.} \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ui,z(0)=dpi,z(t)dtt=0=ℓ(i→z)ifi→z0otherwise.It follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{u}_z(0)$$\end{document}uz(0) contains those entries in the z-th column of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)^\textrm{T}$$\end{document}L(G)T that correspond to the vertices in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)$$\end{document}Vin-(z), which we will denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{v}$$\end{document}v,39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{v}= \textbf{u}_z(0) = \left( \mathcal {L}(G)^\textrm{T}\right) _{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \{z\}]} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v=uz(0)=L(G)T[Vin-(z),{z}].Therefore, we can now rewrite Eqn. 37 as,40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\textbf{u}}_z(s) = \left( s \textbf{I}- \left( \mathcal {L}(G)^\textrm{T}\right) _{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \right) ^{-1} \textbf{v}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u~z(s)=sI-L(G)T[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]-1v.Proposition 2 tells us that the inverse of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}(G)_{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]}$$\end{document}L(G)[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)] exists if, and only if, there exists a spanning forest of G rooted at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)}$$\end{document}Vin-(z)¯.
