Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)}$$\end{document}Vin-(z)¯ contains all the terminal vertices, Z, and every vertex in G leads to some terminal vertex, such a spanning forest must exist (§2.1.1). Because the determinant is a continuous function of the entries of a matrix, Eqn. 40 is well-defined within some neighbourhood of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s = 0$$\end{document}s=0.

For notational convenience in what follows, we introduce the negative transpose of the Laplacian matrix, as41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{L}(G) = -\mathcal {L}(G)^\textrm{T}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(G)=-L(G)T,so that we can rewrite Eqn. 40 as,42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\textbf{u}}_z(s) = \left( s \textbf{I}+ \textbf{L}(G)_{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \right) ^{-1} \textbf{v}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u~z(s)=sI+L(G)[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]-1v.

Calculating moments

With the Laplace transform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{\textbf{u}}_z(s)$$\end{document}u~z(s) at our disposal, we can now start to calculate moments. Although \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{i,z}(t)$$\end{document}ui,z(t) is not a bona fide probability density over t, as noted above, we can still define the quantity corresponding to its r-th moment,43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{i,z}^{(r)} = \int _0^\infty {t^r u_{i,z}(t) \, dt} = \left. \left( -1 \right) ^r \frac{d^r \widetilde{u}_{i,z}(s)}{ds^r} \right| _{s=0}\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μi,z(r)=∫0∞trui,z(t)dt=-1rdru~i,z(s)dsrs=0,where the second equality comes from the well-known property of the Laplace transform that differentiation by s corresponds to multiplication by t. Introducing the vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\mu }^{(r)}_z = (\mu ^{(r)}_{1,z}, \dotsc , \mu ^{(r)}_{N_z,z})^{\textrm{T}}$$\end{document}μz(r)=(μ1,z(r),⋯,μNz,z(r))T and using Eqn. 42, we can write,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varvec{\mu }^{(r)}_z = (-1)^r \left. \frac{d^r}{ds^r} \left( s \textbf{I}+ \textbf{L}(G)_{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \right) ^{-1} \right| _{s=0} \textbf{v}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μz(r)=(-1)rdrdsrsI+L(G)[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]-1s=0v.Recall that, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{M}$$\end{document}M is a square matrix that does not depend on s, then (Bernstein 2009),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d^r}{ds^r} \left( s\textbf{I}+ \textbf{M} \right) ^{-1} = (-1)^r \, r! \left( s\textbf{I}+ \textbf{M} \right) ^{-(r+1)} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}drdsrsI+M-1=(-1)rr!sI+M-(r+1).It follows that,44\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varvec{\mu }^{(r)}_z = r! \left( \textbf{L}(G)_{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \right) ^{-(r+1)} \, \textbf{v}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μz(r)=r!L(G)[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]-(r+1)v.Eqn. 44 immediately reveals how \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\mu }^{(r)}_z$$\end{document}μz(r) can be calculated by appealing to Proposition 2.

Before doing that, it will be helpful for what follows (see Theorem 6) to allow greater generality in the choice of the submatrix of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}(G)$$\end{document}L(G) that we use. Suppose that we choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}U⊆V(G) to be any subset of vertices that contains all the terminal vertices but does not contain any of the non-terminal vertices that lead to z,45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} Z \subseteq U&\subseteq \overline{\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)} \\ \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z) \subseteq {\overline{U}}&\subseteq {\overline{Z}} \,. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Z⊆U⊆Vin-(z)¯Vin-(z)⊆U¯⊆Z¯.Because U contains the terminal SCCs (in this case, terminal vertices) of G, it follows from what was said in §2.1.1 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _U(G) \not = \varnothing $$\end{document}ΦU(G)≠∅. Hence, Proposition 2 tells us that the submatrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{L}(G)_{[{\overline{U}},{\overline{U}}]}$$\end{document}L(G)[U¯,U¯] is invertible. Moreover, suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i > N_z$$\end{document}i>Nz and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \le j \le N_z$$\end{document}1≤j≤Nz, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)$$\end{document}j∈Vin-(z). Then there can be no edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \rightarrow j$$\end{document}i→j: if there were, then, because of our choice of indices, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z) = \{1, \dotsc , N_z, z\}$$\end{document}i∈Vin(z)={1,⋯,Nz,z}. But, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i > N_z$$\end{document}i>Nz, this means that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i = z$$\end{document}i=z, which is terminal, so there can be no edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \rightarrow j$$\end{document}z→j, which yields a contradiction. It follows that this submatrix has a block upper-triangular form,46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{L}(G)_{[{\overline{U}}, {\overline{U}}]} = \left[ \begin{array}{cc} \textbf{A}& \textbf{B}\\ \textbf{0} & \textbf{C}\end{array} \right] \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(G)[U¯,U¯]=AB0C,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{A}= \textbf{L}(G)_{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]}$$\end{document}A=L(G)[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]. Since we know that the block matrix in Eqn. 46 is invertible, we can apply the block matrix inversion formula (Bernstein 2009) to obtain,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \textbf{L}(G)_{[{\overline{U}},{\overline{U}}]} \right) ^{-1} = \left[ \begin{array}{cc} \textbf{A}^{-1} & -\textbf{A}^{-1} \, \textbf{B}\, (\textbf{C}^{-1}) \\ \textbf{0} & \textbf{C}^{-1} \end{array} \right] \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(G)[U¯,U¯]-1=A-1-A-1B(C-1)0C-1.It follows that, provided \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i, j \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)$$\end{document}i,j∈Vin-(z),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \textbf{L}(G)_{[{\overline{U}},{\overline{U}}]} \right) ^{-1}_{i,j} = \textbf{A}^{-1}_{i,j} = \left( \textbf{L}(G)_{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \right) ^{-1}_{i,j} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(G)[U¯,U¯]i,j-1=Ai,j-1=L(G)[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]i,j-1.If we consider Proposition 2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = U$$\end{document}A=U, then, in view of Eqn. 45, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{1, \dotsc , N_z\} \subseteq {\overline{U}}$$\end{document}{1,⋯,Nz}⊆U¯, so that the ordering map, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{{\overline{U}}}: \{1, \dotsc , \# {\overline{U}}\} \rightarrow {\overline{U}}$$\end{document}θU¯:{1,⋯,#U¯}→U¯, is the identity, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{{\overline{U}}}(q) = q$$\end{document}θU¯(q)=q, so long as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z) = \{1, \dotsc , N_z\}$$\end{document}q∈Vin-(z)={1,⋯,Nz}. Accordingly, we can apply Proposition 2 to conclude that,47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \textbf{L}(G)_{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \right) ^{-1}_{i,j} = \frac{w(\Phi _{U \cup \{i\} \rightarrow U \cup \{j\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _U(G))} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(G)[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]i,j-1=w(ΦU∪{i}→U∪{j}(G))w(ΦU(G)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i, j \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)$$\end{document}i,j∈Vin-(z) and U is chosen as in Eqn. 45.

Splitting probabilities and conditional FPT moments

We can now calculate the quantities of interest. First, if we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r = 0$$\end{document}r=0 in Eqn. 43 and recall Eqn. 35, then, for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)$$\end{document}i∈Vin-(z), we have,48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{i,z}^{(0)} = \int _{0}^\infty {u_{i,z}(t) \, dt} = p_{i,z}(\infty ) - p_{i,z}(0) = \pi _{i,z} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μi,z(0)=∫0∞ui,z(t)dt=pi,z(∞)-pi,z(0)=πi,z.Therefore, we can use Eqn. 44 to write,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \pi _{i,z} = \sum _{j \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)}\left( \textbf{L}(G)_{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z),\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \right) _{i,j}^{-1} \, v_j \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}πi,z=∑j∈Vin-(z)L(G)[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]i,j-1vj.Making use of Eqns. 39 and 47, we then see that,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \pi _{i,z} = \sum _{j \in \textrm{V}_{\text {in}}(z)}{\left( \frac{w(\Phi _{U \cup \{i\} \rightarrow U \cup \{j\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _U(G))} \right) \ell (j \rightarrow z)} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}πi,z=∑j∈Vin(z)w(ΦU∪{i}→U∪{j}(G))w(ΦU(G))ℓ(j→z),where we recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{V}_{\text {in}}(z)$$\end{document}Vin(z) is the subset of vertices with edges into z. We can now apply Eqn. 24, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A = U$$\end{document}A=U, to see that,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \pi _{i,z} = \frac{w(\Phi _{(U \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i\} \rightarrow U}(G))}{w(\Phi _U(G))} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}πi,z=w(Φ(U\{z})∪{i}→U(G))w(ΦU(G)).This leads to the first of our main results.

Theorem 4

Let G be a graph whose terminal SCCs are single vertices and let Z be the set of terminal vertices. Choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in Z$$\end{document}z∈Z. Then, for any subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}U⊆V(G) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z \subseteq U \subseteq \overline{\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)}$$\end{document}Z⊆U⊆Vin-(z)¯ and any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)$$\end{document}i∈Vin-(z), the splitting probability from i to z is given by,49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \pi _{i,z} = \frac{w(\Phi _{(U \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i\} \rightarrow U}(G))}{w(\Phi _U(G))}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}πi,z=w(Φ(U\{z})∪{i}→U(G))w(ΦU(G)).

Proof

Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T = \# Z$$\end{document}T=#Z. We are at liberty to re-index the vertices of G so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z = \{N-T+1, \dotsc , N\}$$\end{document}Z={N-T+1,⋯,N} and, for the chosen \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in Z$$\end{document}z∈Z, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z) = \{1, \dotsc , N_z, z\}$$\end{document}Vin(z)={1,⋯,Nz,z}. The preceding calculation then yields Eqn. 49. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The denominator in Eqn. 49 is the weight of all spanning forests of G rooted at U, while the numerator is the weight of the subset of such spanning forests in which there is a path from i to z. Therefore, this ratio is a unitless number that must lie between 0 and 1, as expected for a probability. Moreover, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)$$\end{document}i∈Vin-(z), there is at least one spanning forest that contributes to the numerator, which implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{i,z} \not = 0$$\end{document}πi,z≠0.

Having calculated the splitting probability, we now turn to the moments of the conditional FPT distribution, which we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{(r)}_{c,i,z}$$\end{document}μc,i,z(r). It follows from Eqn. 28 that,50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{c,i,z}^{(r)} = \frac{\mu _{i,z}^{(r)}}{\pi _{i,z}} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μc,i,z(r)=μi,z(r)πi,z.In view of Eqn. 48, we see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{(0)}_{c,i,z} = 1$$\end{document}μc,i,z(0)=1. For the higher moments, we see from Eqn. 44 that,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \varvec{\mu }^{(r)}_z = r! \left( \textbf{L}(G)_{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \right) ^{-r} \varvec{\mu }^{(0)}_z \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μz(r)=r!L(G)[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]-rμz(0),where Eqn. 48 tells us that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\mu }^{(0)}_z = (\pi _{1,z}, \dotsc , \pi _{N_z,z})^{\textrm{T}}$$\end{document}μz(0)=(π1,z,⋯,πNz,z)T. Expanding out the matrix multiplications, we see that,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{i,z}^{(r)} = r! \sum _{(i_1, \dotsc , i_r)} \left( \prod _{j=0}^{r-1}{\left( \textbf{L}(G)_{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \right) _{i_j,i_{j+1}}^{-1}} \right) \pi _{i_r,z} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μi,z(r)=r!∑(i1,⋯,ir)∏j=0r-1L(G)[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]ij,ij+1-1πir,z,where we have set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_0 = i$$\end{document}i0=i and the sum runs over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_1, \dotsc , i_r) \in (\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z))^r$$\end{document}(i1,⋯,ir)∈(Vin-(z))r. It then follows from Eqn. 50 that,51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{c,i,z}^{(r)} = \frac{r!}{\pi _{i,z}} \sum _{(i_1, \dotsc , i_r)} \left( \prod _{j=0}^{r-1}{\left( \textbf{L}(G)_{[\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z), \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)]} \right) _{i_j,i_{j+1}}^{-1}} \right) \pi _{i_r,z} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μc,i,z(r)=r!πi,z∑(i1,⋯,ir)∏j=0r-1L(G)[Vin-(z),Vin-(z)]ij,ij+1-1πir,z.Note that this equation is valid even when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r = 0$$\end{document}r=0. In this case, the sum runs over the empty Cartesian product, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z))^0$$\end{document}(Vin-(z))0, and the term corresponding to the sole 0-tuple contains an empty product, which evaluates to one. Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{i_r,z} = \pi _{i_0,z} = \pi _{i,z}$$\end{document}πir,z=πi0,z=πi,z. Therefore, we obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{c,i,z}^{(0)} = 1$$\end{document}μc,i,z(0)=1, as before.

We can now deduce our second main result. As with Theorem 4, it is convenient to state this in slightly greater generality, in terms of a subset U that satisfies Eqn. 45.

Theorem 5

Let G be a graph whose terminal SCCs are single vertices and let Z be the set of terminal vertices. Choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in Z$$\end{document}z∈Z. Then, for any subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}U⊆V(G) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z \subseteq U \subseteq \overline{\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)}$$\end{document}Z⊆U⊆Vin-(z)¯ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)$$\end{document}i∈Vin-(z), the r-th moment of the conditional FPT from i to z is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu ^{(r)}_{c,i,z} = r! \sum _{(i_1, \dotsc , i_r) \in {\overline{U}}^r}{\left( \prod _{j=0}^{r-1}{\frac{w(\Phi _{U \cup \{i_j\} \rightarrow U \cup \{i_{j+1}\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _U(G))}} \right) \left( \frac{w(\Phi _{(U \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i_r\} \rightarrow U}(G))}{w(\Phi _{(U \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i\} \rightarrow U}(G))} \right) } \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μc,i,z(r)=r!∑(i1,⋯,ir)∈U¯r∏j=0r-1w(ΦU∪{ij}→U∪{ij+1}(G))w(ΦU(G))w(Φ(U\{z})∪{ir}→U(G))w(Φ(U\{z})∪{i}→U(G)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_0 = i$$\end{document}i0=i.

Proof

We undertake a similar re-indexing as in the proof of Theorem 4 and make use of the calculations above. We can rewrite the quantity in Eqn. 51 by using Eqn. 47 to get,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu ^{(r)}_{c,i,z} = r! \sum _{(i_1, \dotsc , i_r)}\left( \prod _{j=0}^{r-1}{\frac{w(\Phi _{U \cup \{i_j\} \rightarrow U \cup \{i_{j+1}\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _U(G))}} \right) \left( \frac{\pi _{i_r,z}}{\pi _{i,z}} \right) \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μc,i,z(r)=r!∑(i1,⋯,ir)∏j=0r-1w(ΦU∪{ij}→U∪{ij+1}(G))w(ΦU(G))πir,zπi,z,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_1, \dotsc , i_r) \in (\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z))^r$$\end{document}(i1,⋯,ir)∈(Vin-(z))r. We can then use Theorem 4 to rewrite the ratio of splitting probabilities to get,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu ^{(r)}_{c,i,z} = r! \sum _{(i_1, \dotsc , i_r)}\left( \prod _{j=0}^{r-1}{\frac{w(\Phi _{U \cup \{i_j\} \rightarrow U \cup \{i_{j+1}\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _U(G))}} \right) \left( \frac{w(\Phi _{(U \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i_r\} \rightarrow U}(G))}{w(\Phi _{(U \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i\} \rightarrow U}(G))} \right) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μc,i,z(r)=r!∑(i1,⋯,ir)∏j=0r-1w(ΦU∪{ij}→U∪{ij+1}(G))w(ΦU(G))w(Φ(U\{z})∪{ir}→U(G))w(Φ(U\{z})∪{i}→U(G)).This is the quantity in the statement of the Theorem, except that the summation is taken over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_1, \dotsc , i_r) \in (\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z))^r$$\end{document}(i1,⋯,ir)∈(Vin-(z))r. To see that we can convert the sum to one that runs over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{U}}^r$$\end{document}U¯r, note that the r-fold product in the numerator of the first parenthetical term,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \prod _{j=0}^{r-1}{w(\Phi _{U \cup \{i_j\} \rightarrow U \cup \{i_{j+1}\}}(G))}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∏j=0r-1w(ΦU∪{ij}→U∪{ij+1}(G)),is nonzero if, and only if, there are paths\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} i_0 = i \leadsto i_1, \qquad i_1 \leadsto i_2, \qquad \dotsc , \qquad i_{r-1} \leadsto i_r \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}i0=i⇝i1,i1⇝i2,⋯,ir-1⇝ir.Furthermore, the weight in the numerator of the second parenthetical term is nonzero if, and only if, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_r \leadsto z$$\end{document}ir⇝z. Therefore, the summand corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_1, \dotsc , i_r)$$\end{document}(i1,⋯,ir) is zero if at least one vertex in the r-tuple does not have a path to z. In view of Eqn. 45, any elements in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{U}}$$\end{document}U¯ that are not in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}}^{-}(z)$$\end{document}Vin-(z) do not contribute to the sum. Hence, we are at liberty to take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_1, \dotsc , i_r) \in {\overline{U}}^r$$\end{document}(i1,⋯,ir)∈U¯r, as stated. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The r-th moment of the conditional FPT, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{(r)}_{c,i,z}$$\end{document}μc,i,z(r), as given by the formula in Theorem 5, has units of (time)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^r$$\end{document}r. It is instructive to see how this arises on the right-hand side of the formula. Recall that a spanning forest F has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N - \#\mathcal {R}(F)$$\end{document}N-#R(F) edges. Hence, the second parenthetical term in the right-hand sum is unitless. In the first term, the weight in the numerator incurs one fewer edge label, and corresponding unit of (time)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{-1}$$\end{document}-1, than the weight in the denominator, so that the ratio has units of time. It follows that the right-hand side as a whole has units of (time)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^r$$\end{document}r, as required.

We also note that the formula for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{(r)}_{c,i,z}$$\end{document}μc,i,z(r) in Theorem 5 shares with that for the steady-state probabilities in Eqn. 5 the property of being a manifestly positive rational algebraic function of the edge labels.

As the mean is often of interest, it may be helpful to extricate this as a separate result. Under the same conditions as Theorem 5,52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{c,i,z}^{(1)} = \sum _{j \in {\overline{U}}}{\left( \frac{w(\Phi _{U \cup \{i\} \rightarrow U \cup \{j\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _U(G))} \right) \left( \frac{w(\Phi _{(U \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{j\} \rightarrow U}(G))}{w(\Phi _{(U \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i\} \rightarrow U}(G))} \right) } \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μc,i,z(1)=∑j∈U¯w(ΦU∪{i}→U∪{j}(G))w(ΦU(G))w(Φ(U\{z})∪{j}→U(G))w(Φ(U\{z})∪{i}→U(G)).

The unconditional FPT to Z

It follows readily from Eqn. 30 that the r-th moment of the FPT distribution to reach any of the terminal vertices in Z from i is given by,53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{i,Z}^{(r)} = \sum _{z \in \mathcal {Z}(i)} \mu _{c,i,z}^{(r)} \, \pi _{i,z} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μi,Z(r)=∑z∈Z(i)μc,i,z(r)πi,z,where we have made use of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}(i) \subseteq Z$$\end{document}Z(i)⊆Z, as introduced in Eqn. 31. Eqn. 53 allows us to deduce our third main result.

Theorem 6

Let G be a graph whose terminal SCCs are single vertices and let Z be the set of terminal vertices. The r-th moment of the FPT from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in {\overline{Z}}$$\end{document}i∈Z¯ to Z is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{i,Z}^{(r)} = r! \sum _{(i_1, \dotsc , i_r) \in {\overline{Z}}^r}{\left( \prod _{j=0}^{r-1}{\frac{w(\Phi _{Z \cup \{i_j\} \rightarrow Z \cup \{i_{j+1}\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _Z(G))}} \right) }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μi,Z(r)=r!∑(i1,⋯,ir)∈Z¯r∏j=0r-1w(ΦZ∪{ij}→Z∪{ij+1}(G))w(ΦZ(G)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_0 = i$$\end{document}i0=i.

Proof

Let us take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U = Z$$\end{document}U=Z and apply Theorems 4 and 5 to Eqn. 53 to obtain the following formula for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{(r)}_{i,Z}$$\end{document}μi,Z(r),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} r! \sum _{z \in \mathcal {Z}(i)}{\sum _{(i_1, \dotsc , i_r) \in {\overline{Z}}^r}{\left( \prod _{j=0}^{r-1}{\frac{w(\Phi _{Z \cup \{i_j\} \rightarrow Z \cup \{i_{j+1}\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _Z(G))}} \right) \left( \frac{w(\Phi _{(Z \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i_r\} \rightarrow Z}(G))}{w(\Phi _Z(G))} \right) }} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}r!∑z∈Z(i)∑(i1,⋯,ir)∈Z¯r∏j=0r-1w(ΦZ∪{ij}→Z∪{ij+1}(G))w(ΦZ(G))w(Φ(Z\{z})∪{ir}→Z(G))w(ΦZ(G)).The r-fold product within the inner sum does not depend on z, so we can rearrange the formula to give,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} r! \sum _{(i_1, \dotsc , i_r) \in {\overline{Z}}^r} \left( \prod _{j=0}^{r-1}{\frac{w(\Phi _{Z \cup \{i_j\} \rightarrow Z \cup \{i_{j+1}\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _Z(G))}} \right) \left( \sum _{z \in \mathcal {Z}(i)}{\frac{w(\Phi _{(Z \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i_r\} \rightarrow Z}(G))}{w(\Phi _Z(G))}} \right) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}r!∑(i1,⋯,ir)∈Z¯r∏j=0r-1w(ΦZ∪{ij}→Z∪{ij+1}(G))w(ΦZ(G))∑z∈Z(i)w(Φ(Z\{z})∪{ir}→Z(G))w(ΦZ(G)).As in the proof of Theorem 5, the r-fold product of weights in the numerator of the first parenthetical term is nonzero if, and only if, there are paths54\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} i_0 = i \leadsto i_1, \qquad i_1 \leadsto i_2, \qquad \dotsc , \qquad i_{r-1} \leadsto i_r \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}i0=i⇝i1,i1⇝i2,⋯,ir-1⇝ir.Furthermore, the numerator in the second parenthetical term is nonzero if, and only if, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_r \leadsto z$$\end{document}ir⇝z, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \mathcal {Z}(i_r)$$\end{document}z∈Z(ir). Because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\leadsto $$\end{document}⇝ is transitive, Eqn. 54 tells us that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \leadsto i_r$$\end{document}i⇝ir. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z' \in \mathcal {Z}(i_r)$$\end{document}z′∈Z(ir), so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_r \leadsto z'$$\end{document}ir⇝z′, then it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \leadsto z'$$\end{document}i⇝z′, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z' \in \mathcal {Z}(i)$$\end{document}z′∈Z(i). Hence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {Z}(i_r) \subseteq \mathcal {Z}(i)$$\end{document}Z(ir)⊆Z(i) for any r-tuple for which the corresponding summand above is nonzero. Therefore, we can rewrite the second parenthetical expression as,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{z \in \mathcal {Z}(i_r)} \frac{w(\Phi _{(Z \setminus \{z\}) \cup \{i_r\} \rightarrow Z}(G))}{w(\Phi _Z(G))} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑z∈Z(ir)w(Φ(Z\{z})∪{ir}→Z(G))w(ΦZ(G)).But now we see from Theorem 4 that this expression is nothing other than the total sum of splitting probabilities from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_r$$\end{document}ir,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{z \in \mathcal {Z}(i_r)} \pi _{i_r,z} = 1 \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑z∈Z(ir)πir,z=1,from which the result follows. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

In a similar fashion to the formula in Theorem 5, the formula in Theorem 6 has the required units of (time)\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{r}$$\end{document}r and is a manifestly positive rational algebraic function of the edge labels.

As before, it may be helpful to extricate from Theorem 6 the mean FPT from i to Z, which is given by,55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{i,Z}^{(1)} = \sum _{j \in {\overline{Z}}}{\frac{w(\Phi _{Z \cup \{i\} \rightarrow Z \cup \{j\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _Z(G))}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μi,Z(1)=∑j∈Z¯w(ΦZ∪{i}→Z∪{j}(G))w(ΦZ(G)).

A single terminal vertex

The case of a single terminal vertex, z, is often encountered and it may be helpful to see what the results above yield in this case. Evidently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (z) = \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}Vin(z)=V(G). Taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U = \{z\}$$\end{document}U={z}, as we would expect, Theorem 4 implies that the splitting probability from every vertex is one,56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \pi _{i,z} = \frac{w(\Phi _{\{i\} \rightarrow \{z\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _{\{z\}}(G))} = 1 \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}πi,z=w(Φ{i}→{z}(G))w(Φ{z}(G))=1,since every spanning tree in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\{z\}}(G)$$\end{document}Φ{z}(G) has a path from i to z. It is also clear that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{c,i,z}^{(r)} = \mu _{i,z}^{(r)} = \mu ^{(r)}_{i,\{z\}} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μc,i,z(r)=μi,z(r)=μi,{z}(r),where the first equality follows from Eqn. 50 and the second from Eqn. 53. We can summarise this as follows.

Proposition 7

Let G be a graph with one terminal vertex, z. Then the r-th moment of the FPT from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}i∈V(G) to z is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{i,z}^{(r)} = r! \sum _{(i_1, \dotsc , i_r) \in \left( \mathcal {V}(G) \setminus \{z\} \right) ^r} \left( \prod _{j=0}^{r-1}{\frac{w(\Phi _{\{i_j,z\} \rightarrow \{i_{j+1},z\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _{\{z\}}(G))}} \right) \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μi,z(r)=r!∑(i1,⋯,ir)∈V(G)\{z}r∏j=0r-1w(Φ{ij,z}→{ij+1,z}(G))w(Φ{z}(G)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_0 = i$$\end{document}i0=i.

As before, it may be helpful to extricate from Proposition 7 the result for the mean FPT from i to z,57\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _{i,z}^{(1)} = \sum _{j \in \mathcal {V}(G) \setminus \{z\}}{\frac{w(\Phi _{\{i,z\} \rightarrow \{j,z\}}(G))}{w(\Phi _{\{z\}}(G))}}\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μi,z(1)=∑j∈V(G)\{z}w(Φ{i,z}→{j,z}(G))w(Φ{z}(G)).

FPTs on general graphs

Fig. 3The edge removal procedure for FPT calculations on general graphs. The graph, G, on the left has five terminal SCCs, shown within dashed ovals, and two non-terminal SCCs. The source vertex i and the target vertex v are labelled. Note that v is in a terminal SCC. The edge removal procedure described in the text yields the graph, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G], in the shaded region on the right. As implied by Lemma 8, the terminal SCCs of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G] are all singletons, and are shown in black

The edge removal procedure for FPT calculations on general graphs. The graph, G, on the left has five terminal SCCs, shown within dashed ovals, and two non-terminal SCCs. The source vertex i and the target vertex v are labelled. Note that v is in a terminal SCC. The edge removal procedure described in the text yields the graph, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G], in the shaded region on the right. As implied by Lemma 8, the terminal SCCs of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G] are all singletons, and are shown in black

Our main results above, Theorems 4, 5 and 6, were formulated for graphs whose terminal SCCs are singletons. Here, we show how these results can be extended to more general situations through a process of removing an appropriate set of edges.

Let G be a connected graph with any number of terminal SCCs, each of which may contain any number of vertices, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(\cdot )$$\end{document}X(·) be the associated Markov process. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}v∈V(G) be any vertex, which need not lie within a terminal SCC. We want to calculate the splitting probability and conditional FPT to v from some vertex, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (v)$$\end{document}i∈Vin(v). To determine these quantities, first define a modified graph in which the outgoing edges from v are removed, along with the outgoing edges from any vertex in a terminal SCC, unless that SCC contains v itself. More precisely, the outgoing edges are removed from the set of vertices,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \left\{ v \right\} \cup \left( \bigcup _{C \in \mathcal {T}(G)\,,\, v \not \in C}{C} \right) \,. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v∪⋃C∈T(G),v∉CC.This procedure is illustrated in Fig. 3. As shown in the figure, it may yield a graph with several connected components. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G] denote the connected component of the modified graph that contains v, as shown with a shaded background in Fig. 3.

Lemma 8

The terminal SCCs of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G] are all singletons. Furthermore, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (v)$$\end{document}i∈Vin(v), then the trajectory probabilities from i defined in Eqn. 25 are identical in G and in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G], so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p^G_{i,v}(t) = p^{{\mathcal {F}}_v[G]}_{i,v}(t)$$\end{document}pi,vG(t)=pi,vFv[G](t).

Proof

The edge-removal procedure only removes edges, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {V}({\mathcal {F}}_v[G]) \subseteq \mathcal {V}(G)$$\end{document}V(Fv[G])⊆V(G) and the SCCs of G can only break up into SCCs of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G]. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \in \mathcal {C}(G) \setminus \mathcal {T}(G)$$\end{document}C∈C(G)\T(G) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \not \ni v$$\end{document}C∌v, then the procedure does nothing to it. Since C is non-terminal, it must have edges leaving it that are not removed by the procedure, so C remains a non-terminal SCC of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G]. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \in \mathcal {T}(G)$$\end{document}C∈T(G) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \not \ni v$$\end{document}C∌v, then the procedure disintegrates it so that only those vertices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \in C$$\end{document}j∈C with an incoming edge from outside C are retained in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G]. Each such j becomes a singleton terminal SCC, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{j\} \in \mathcal {T}({\mathcal {F}}_v[G])$$\end{document}{j}∈T(Fv[G]). Finally, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \ni v$$\end{document}C∋v, then the procedure removes the outgoing edges from v and thereby breaks up the strongly connected structure of C so that v becomes a terminal SCC, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{v\} \in \mathcal {T}({\mathcal {F}}_v[G])$$\end{document}{v}∈T(Fv[G]). Accordingly, the only terminal SCCs of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G] are singletons.

For the second claim, we will argue as we did for Eqn. 25. Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \mathcal {V}_{\text {in}} (v)$$\end{document}i∈Vin(v), consider stochastically generating a large finite ensemble of unbounded trajectories of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X(\cdot )$$\end{document}X(·) starting from i. With probability one, each of these trajectories will either reach v at some time or it will not. If a trajectory reaches v for the first time, truncate it at that time and extend it indefinitely while remaining at v. If a trajectory does not reach v, it will eventually reach a terminal SCC, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C \in \mathcal {T}(G)$$\end{document}C∈T(G), which does not contain v. In this case, truncate the trajectory at the first vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j \in C$$\end{document}j∈C that is reached and extend it indefinitely while remaining at j. From the construction above, we see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{j\} \in \mathcal {T}({\mathcal {F}}_v[G])$$\end{document}{j}∈T(Fv[G]). Each truncated trajectory is a trajectory of the Markov process associated with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G] and this truncation process gives rise to a bona fide ensemble of trajectories in this latter Markov process. As the sizes of the two ensembles go to infinity, the probabilities of the original and truncated trajectories become identical, from which the second claim follows. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

In view of Lemma 8, the splitting probabilities and the moments of the conditional FPT distribution from i to v in G can now be calculated by applying Theorems 4 and 5, respectively, to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {F}}_v[G]$$\end{document}Fv[G].

Examples

In a prequel to the present paper, we summarised the main results discussed above without providing proofs and also worked through some example calculations for pipeline graphs (Nam and Gunawardena 2023, §2.4). These graphs consist of sequences of vertices with edges only between consecutive vertices (Fig. 4). The corresponding Markov processes have been widely studied in the context of single-molecule enzyme kinetics (Shaevitz et al. 2005; Kou et al. 2005; Chemla et al. 2008; Garai et al. 2009; Moffitt et al. 2010; Moffitt and Bustamante 2014). Pipeline graphs are particularly amenable to spanning forest enumeration, and we showed in Nam and Gunawardena (2023) how our results provide a systematic way to recover findings in the literature. Here, we will examine three examples, two pipeline graphs (Fig. 4) and a butterfly graph (Fig. 5), to illustrate in more detail how our formulas can be worked out in practice.

Pipeline graphs

Consider first the pipeline graph in Fig. 4A, consisting of a pair of vertices linked by reversible edges, leading to a terminal vertex. This graph has been used to model a single catalytic cycle of a Michaelis–Menten enzyme mechanism (Shaevitz et al. 2005; Kou et al. 2005; Moffitt et al. 2010; Moffitt and Bustamante 2014), with the vertices representing enzyme states and the edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \rightarrow 2$$\end{document}1→2 corresponding to substrate binding, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \rightarrow 1$$\end{document}2→1 to substrate unbinding and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \rightarrow 3$$\end{document}2→3 to catalysis. The FPT from 1 to 3 is then a measure of the catalytic rate. Since there is only a single terminal vertex, we can use Proposition 7 to calculate the corresponding moments, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{(r)}_{1,3}$$\end{document}μ1,3(r), where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} Z = \{ 3 \} \qquad \text{ and } \qquad \mathcal {V}_{\text{ in }}^{-} (3) = \{ 1, 2 \} \,. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Z={3}andVin-(3)={1,2}.We will omit the name of the graph in what follows, as it is clear from the context.Fig. 4(A) A pipeline graph on three vertices, as discussed further in the text. The spanning forests are shown below. Red signifies a root vertex. (B) A pipeline graph on five vertices, as discussed further in the text, with the relevant spanning forests shown below. Red signifies a root vertex

(A) A pipeline graph on three vertices, as discussed further in the text. The spanning forests are shown below. Red signifies a root vertex. (B) A pipeline graph on five vertices, as discussed further in the text, with the relevant spanning forests shown below. Red signifies a root vertex

According to Proposition 7, we need to calculate a sum over the r-tuples,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (i_1, \dotsc , i_r) \in \left\{ 1,2 \right\} ^r \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(i1,⋯,ir)∈1,2r,using spanning trees and forests drawn from the sets shown in Fig. 4A. There is only a single spanning tree rooted at 3, which contributes a weight of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_1k_3$$\end{document}k1k3. As for the forests, the subsets required have the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\{i_j,3\} \rightarrow \{i_{j+1},3\}}$$\end{document}Φ{ij,3}→{ij+1,3}, in which there is a path from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_j$$\end{document}ij to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{j+1}$$\end{document}ij+1 in the forest. Each such forest has only a single edge, and it follows from Fig. 4A that the forest weights are as given in the following table,58\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{|r|c|c|} \hline ~ & i_{j+1} = 1 & i_{j+1} = 2 \\ \hline i_j = 1 & k_2 + k_3 & k_1 \\ i_j = 2 & k_2 & k_1 \\ \hline \end{array} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ij+1=1ij+1=2ij=1k2+k3k1ij=2k2k1.The r-tuple, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i_1, \dotsc , i_r)$$\end{document}(i1,⋯,ir), provides a code for reading off the corresponding product of forest weights, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w(\Phi _{\{i_0,3\} \rightarrow \{i_1,3\}}) \cdots w(\Phi _{\{i_{r-1},3\} \rightarrow \{i_{r},3\}})$$\end{document}w(Φ{i0,3}→{i1,3})⋯w(Φ{ir-1,3}→{ir,3}), in the formula in Proposition 7.

For the mean FPT, we see from Eqn. 58 that,59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu ^{(1)}_{1,3} = \sum _{i_1 \in \{1,2\}} \frac{w(\Phi _{\{1,3\} \rightarrow \{i_1,3\}})}{w(\Phi _{\{3\}})} = \frac{\left( k_2 + k_3 \right) + k_1}{k_1 k_3} \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ1,3(1)=∑i1∈{1,2}w(Φ{1,3}→{i1,3})w(Φ{3})=k2+k3+k1k1k3.This formula is intuitively reasonable: if either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_1$$\end{document}k1 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_3$$\end{document}k3 becomes comparatively large, the mean FPT approaches the reciprocal of the other rate; if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_2$$\end{document}k2 becomes comparatively large, the mean FPT increases in proportion.

For the second moment, we see from Eqn. 58 that,60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \mu ^{(2)}_{1,3}&= 2\sum _{(i_1,i_2) \in \{1,2\}^2}\left( \prod _{j = 0}^1 \frac{w(\Phi _{\{i_j,3\} \rightarrow \{i_{j+1},3\}})}{w(\Phi _{\{3\}})}\right) \\&= \frac{2 \left( \left( k_2+k_3 \right) ^2 + \left( k_2+k_3 \right) k_1 + k_1 k_2 + k_1^2 \right) }{\left( k_1 k_3 \right) ^2} \,. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μ1,3(2)=2∑(i1,i2)∈{1,2}2∏j=01w(Φ{ij,3}→{ij+1,3})w(Φ{3})=2k2+k32+k2+k3k1+k1k2+k12k1k32.The higher moments may be calculated in a similar way. It follows from Eqns. 59 and 60 that the quantity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{(2)}_{1,3} - 2(\mu ^{(1)}_{1,3})^2$$\end{document}μ1,3(2)-2(μ1,3(1))2, which is similar to the variance, simplifies to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-2/(k_1 k_3)$$\end{document}-2/(k1k3). This quantity is independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_2$$\end{document}k2, which may be helpful in experimentally testing whether an observed system can be reasonably described by the graph in Fig. 4A. It is plausible that similar polynomial simplifications exist between the higher moments, but this question lies outside the scope of the present paper.

We now consider the pipeline graph in Fig. 4B with five vertices, of which 4 and 5 are both terminal. Here,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Z = \{4, 5\} \quad \text {and} \quad {\mathcal {V}}_{\text {in}}^{-}(4)={\mathcal {V}}_{\text {in}}^{-}(5)= \{ 1, 2, 3 \} \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Z={4,5}andVin-(4)=Vin-(5)={1,2,3},and we will take the source vertex to be the central vertex 2. This graph was used by Terrell Hill to illustrate another method for calculating FPTs (Hill 1988), to which we will return in the Discussion, and we make use of a similar pipeline graph with 23 vertices in our analysis of the CRISPR–Cas9 system (Nam and Gunawardena 2025). Hill’s method permits calculation of the splitting probabilities, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{2,4}$$\end{document}π2,4 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _{2,5}$$\end{document}π2,5, and the mean unconditional FPT to any terminal vertex, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{(1)}_{2,\{4,5\}}$$\end{document}μ2,{4,5}(1), but not the mean conditional FPTs to specific terminal vertices, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{(1)}_{c,2,4}$$\end{document}μc,2,4(1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu ^{(1)}_{c,2,5}$$\end{document}μc,2,5(1), which Hill calculated by “a separate stochastic argument (omitted).” In contrast, we can calculate all these quantities with our results, using the spanning forests shown in Fig. 4B.

Hill used a different notation, with the Markov transition rates given in the following table,61along with the symbols (Hill 1988, Eqn. 11),62\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}
